And it's not just that specific file, either. It's like... anything even remotely connected to it. \n\nI've been letting all my calls about any of our industrial accounts just go straight to voicemail. Which is terrible, I know, because I pride myself on being incredibly responsive to my clients. Usually I return a call within the hour, always. But right now, even just seeing an email pop up on my phone with a subject line about a commercial site visit or a factory policy... my stomach just completely drops. It's this awful, heavy feeling, like I'm on a roller coaster right before it goes over the edge. I just can't bring myself to click on them. I've been marking them as unread and telling myself I'll deal with them later, but then later comes and I just... I can't do it. I just can't open them. It's like my brain is just screaming at me to stay away from anything that reminds me of that place or that kind of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're experiencing any moments where you feel detached from yourself or your surroundings, like you're observing things from outside your body?\n\nPatient: Um, detached? Like an out-of-body thing? I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I've heard people talk about that, you know, in movies or whatever, where they float above themselves or feel like they're in a dream, but I haven't really had anything like that. I'm definitely very stuck in my own body right now, unfortunately. I mean, I feel every little thing. Every time my heart races or my stomach drops or my palms get sweaty, I am acutely aware of it. It's almost the opposite of being detached, really. I feel like I'm hyper-attached to every single uncomfortable physical sensation I'm having. \n\nLike yesterday, for example, I was just sitting in my kitchen, trying to drink some chamomile tea because my mom always said chamomile is good for your nerves, right? And I was just holding the mug, trying to focus on the warmth of it, but instead, I just felt this intense tightness in my chest, and my breathing felt so shallow, and I was just so hyper-focused on how awful I felt in my own skin. So no, I wouldn't say I feel outside of myself at all. I just feel... trapped inside a version of myself that is constantly on edge and exhausted. I just wish I could detach, honestly, just for a little while, just to get a break from feeling so keyed up all the time. But no, I haven't experienced anything like what you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I... no, I wouldn't say guilty. I mean, I've definitely thought about everything leading up to it, playing it over and over in my head. Like, I keep wondering if I had parked in the front lot instead of pulling around to the side, or if I had spent ten more minutes talking to the facility manager about their sprinkler maintenance logs before heading back to the loading dock... would things have been different? Would I have not been right there when it happened? But I don't feel like it was my fault, if that's what you mean. \n\nI know I was just doing my job. I was just following my standard inspection checklist, the exact same one I've used for, gosh, probably eighty or ninety site visits over the last three years. It's just a standard form, you know? You check the electrical panels, you check the fire exits, you check the storage of combustible materials. I was doing exactly what I was supposed to be doing, exactly where I was supposed to be doing it. So, logically, I know there's no reason for me to feel guilty. I didn't cause the blast. I was just... there. \n\nIt's more just this overwhelming sense of frustration about the missing time, like I told you. And maybe a little bit of anger, honestly, that this normal, routine Tuesday morning turned into this huge thing that's completely derailed my life right now. But guilt? No. I don't feel like I did anything wrong. I just feel like I was in the wrong place at the absolute wrong time, and now I'm the one having to deal with the fallout of it, sitting here feeling like a nervous wreck every time I even think about my job. But I definitely don't feel like I'm to blame for any of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to concentrate on your daily activities because of worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um, no, actually. I mean, not on my normal, everyday stuff. It's weird, right? You'd think with everything else going on, my brain would just be complete mush, but I've actually been... well, if anything, I've been throwing myself into my daily chores, and my focus there is perfectly fine. \n\nLike yesterday, I decided to deep clean my entire apartment. And I don't just mean a quick vacuum and wiping down the counters. I mean, I pulled the refrigerator out from the wall to sweep behind it, I scrubbed the grout in the bathroom tile with this little old toothbrush I keep just for cleaning, I reorganized my entire pantry... which honestly took like three hours because I decided to sort everything by expiration date and then by food group. I had all these canned goods from last year, like four different types of diced tomatoes, that I needed to sort through and consolidate. \n\nAnd while I was doing all of that, I was completely focused. I was listening to this podcast about historical European architecture—which is completely unrelated to my job, obviously, since I just underwrite modern commercial properties, I don't design them—and I was following along with every single word the host was saying. I didn't lose my train of thought or forget what I was doing or get distracted by worrying about the incident. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I'm having trouble concentrating on my regular daily activities. As long as it has absolutely nothing to do with work or... or that inspection, my focus is actually completely normal. It's almost like my brain is just so grateful to have something mundane to latch onto, you know? Like, \"Oh, thank goodness, we're just organizing spices right now, let's put a hundred percent of our energy into making sure the paprika is next to the cayenne.\" So yeah, the anxiety isn't really getting in the way of my daily routine stuff at home at all. I can read my books, I can cook complicated recipes, I can pay my personal utility bills... I'm totally dialed in on all of that. It's just the work stuff I can't look at.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself becoming excessively anxious when you encounter reminders of the warehouse inspection, such as seeing a similar building or hearing a loud noise?\n\nPatient: Well, reminders... um, it's not even necessarily seeing a similar building or hearing a loud noise. I mean, I've honestly been avoiding industrial parks and loud places anyway, so I haven't really tested that out too much. It's actually the paperwork that does it. \n\nLike, during the day, I'll just be trying to catch up on things, right? I'll be reviewing a standard policy or looking at a routine loss report for a completely different client—you know, just normal commercial property underwriting stuff, maybe a minor water damage claim or a basic liability renewal for a strip mall or something totally mundane like that. I'm just looking at the text on my screen. \n\nBut out of nowhere, I just suddenly get hit with this incredibly sharp image of what happened. It's so vivid, it's almost like a flash but it just takes over my whole brain. I see the worker on the floor, and all that soot... God, the soot hanging in the air, it's like I can almost taste it and smell the smoke all over again. And it feels like my body just reacts before I can even stop it or tell myself that I'm safe in my own apartment. \n\nMy heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and I get that awful sweaty, tight feeling, and I just completely freeze up. It's so incredibly frustrating because I'm just trying to read a completely unrelated document about a building in, like, Ohio or somewhere. It has nothing to do with warehouses! But boom, suddenly I'm right back there seeing the soot and the worker on the ground. I try to push it away, I really do. I'll minimize the window and just stare at my desktop wallpaper—it's a picture of my cat, Barnaby—but it takes me a good fifteen or twenty minutes to get my breathing back to normal and convince my body that we aren't in danger. So yeah, I guess those work documents are my reminders, and I definitely get... well, excessively anxious is probably an understatement when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the warehouse inspection incident as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Reliving it? Like, like I'm actually back there? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I told you about the images, right? When I'm looking at the paperwork and I get that really sharp flash of the soot and the worker on the floor. That's definitely distressing, and it makes my heart race and my palms sweat, but I know I'm sitting at my desk in my apartment. I know I'm looking at my computer monitor. \n\nIt's not like... it's not like one of those movies where the character suddenly thinks they're back in a war zone and they dive under the table to take cover. I'm not hallucinating or losing touch with reality. I know I'm safe at home with Barnaby. It's just a really, really vivid memory that forces its way in. It's like my brain pulls up a high-definition photograph of that exact moment and shoves it right in front of my face, and my body reacts to the stress of that memory. But I don't feel like I've actually time-traveled back to that Tuesday morning. \n\nI'm very aware that it's just a memory, even if it's an incredibly intrusive and upsetting one. So, no, I don't feel like it's happening all over again in real time. It's just... it's just a very persistent, very detailed recollection that I can't seem to control when I'm trying to review a simple liability policy or a loss report. But I'm definitely still in the present moment when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific people, places, or activities that you're avoiding because they remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Well, avoiding... yeah, absolutely. I mean, I mentioned earlier that I haven't been going near industrial parks, but it's really the work itself that I'm completely dodging. It's terrible, I know it's terrible for my job, but I just can't help it. \n\nLike, the specific warehouse file from the inspection? I haven't opened it once since that day. It's just sitting there on my desktop, in this little folder I made called \"Pending Underwriting,\" and every time my mouse even hovers near it, I feel sick. And it's not just that one file, either. It's spilled over into everything related to it. Any time my phone rings and the caller ID shows it's a broker calling about an industrial account—like, any industrial account, even a totally standard manufacturing facility or a distribution center that has nothing to do with the one I visited—I just let it go straight to voicemail. I can't answer it. I'll sit there and watch the little red light blink on my desk phone, and I'll tell myself, \"Just pick it up, it's just a conversation about sprinkler systems and square footage,\" but my hand just won't move. \n\nAnd the emails... oh my gosh, the emails are almost worse because they just pile up in my inbox, right? I'll see a subject line that says something like \"Warehouse Liability Renewal\" or \"Industrial Property Quote,\" and my stomach just completely drops. Like, physically drops, like I'm on a roller coaster that just went over the edge. I get this awful, heavy feeling right in the pit of my stomach, and I just quickly click on a different email, maybe something about a small retail shop or a suburban office building, just to get it off my screen. \n\nI know I'm going to have to deal with it eventually, because my manager is going to start asking why these accounts are stalling, and I don't know what I'm going to tell her. \"Sorry, Susan, I can't underwrite warehouses anymore because they make my stomach hurt\"? She's not going to accept that. But right now, I'm just doing everything in my power to avoid anything that even tangentially reminds me of that industrial space. It's exhausting, honestly, trying to navigate my own inbox like it's a minefield. So yes, specific activities—definitely opening those files and taking those calls. I'm avoiding them like the plague.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um, lost interest? No, not really. I made this really complicated mushroom risotto from scratch on Saturday, stirring it constantly for like forty-five minutes just to get the arborio rice perfectly creamy, and I really enjoyed the process and eating it afterward. \n\nAnd I still like watching my shows. It's almost like my brain is just so grateful to have something mundane to latch onto, you know? Like, \"Oh, thank goodness, we're just organizing spices right now, let's put a hundred percent of our energy into making sure the paprika is next to the cayenne.\" So yeah, the anxiety isn't really getting in the way of my daily routine stuff at home at all. I can read my books, I can cook complicated recipes, I can pay my personal utility bills... I'm totally dialed in on all of that. It's just the work stuff I can't look at.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself becoming excessively anxious when you encounter reminders of the warehouse inspection, such as seeing a similar building or hearing a loud noise?\n\nPatient: Well, reminders... um, it's not even necessarily seeing a similar building or hearing a loud noise. I mean, I've honestly been avoiding industrial parks and loud places anyway, so I haven't really tested that out too much. It's actually the paperwork that does it. \n\nLike, during the day, I'll just be trying to catch up on things, right? I'll be reviewing a standard policy or looking at a routine loss report for a completely different client—you know, just normal commercial property underwriting stuff, maybe a minor water damage claim or a basic liability renewal for a strip mall or something totally mundane like that. I'm just looking at the text on my screen. \n\nBut out of nowhere, I just suddenly get hit with this incredibly sharp image of what happened. It's so vivid, it's almost like a flash but it just takes over my whole brain. I see the worker on the floor, and all that soot... God, the soot hanging in the air, it's like I can almost taste it and smell the smoke all over again. And it feels like my body just reacts before I can even stop it or tell myself that I'm safe in my own apartment. \n\nMy heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and I get that awful sweaty, tight feeling, and I just completely freeze up. It's so incredibly frustrating because I'm just trying to read a completely unrelated document about a building in, like, Ohio or somewhere. It has nothing to do with warehouses! But boom, suddenly I'm right back there seeing the soot and the worker on the ground. I try to push it away, I really do. I'll minimize the window and just stare at my desktop wallpaper—it's a picture of my cat, Barnaby—but it takes me a good fifteen or twenty minutes to get my breathing back to normal and convince my body that we aren't in danger. So yeah, I guess those work documents are my reminders, and I definitely get... well, excessively anxious is probably an understatement when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the warehouse inspection incident as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Reliving it? Like, like I'm actually back there? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I told you about the images, right? When I'm looking at the paperwork and I get that really sharp flash of the soot and the worker on the floor. That's definitely distressing, and it makes my heart race and my palms sweat, but I know I'm sitting at my desk in my apartment. I know I'm looking at my computer monitor. \n\nIt's not like... it's not like one of those movies where the character suddenly thinks they're back in a war zone and they dive under the table to take cover. I'm not hallucinating or losing touch with reality. I know I'm safe at home with Barnaby. It's just a really, really vivid memory that forces its way in. It's like my brain pulls up a high-definition photograph of that exact moment and shoves it right in front of my face, and my body reacts to the stress of that memory. But I don't feel like I've actually time-traveled back to that Tuesday morning. \n\nI'm very aware that it's just a memory, even if it's an incredibly intrusive and upsetting one. So, no, I don't feel like it's happening all over again in real time. It's just... it's just a very persistent, very detailed recollection that I can't seem to control when I'm trying to review a simple liability policy or a loss report. But I'm definitely still in the present moment when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific people, places, or activities that you're avoiding because they remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Well, avoiding... yeah, absolutely. I mean, I mentioned earlier that I haven't been going near industrial parks, but it's really the work itself that I'm completely dodging. It's terrible, I know it's terrible for my job, but I just can't help it. \n\nLike, the specific warehouse file from the inspection? I haven't opened it once since that day. It's just sitting there on my desktop, in this little folder I made called \"Pending Underwriting,\" and every time my mouse even hovers near it, I feel sick. And it's not just that one file, either. It's spilled over into everything related to it. Any time my phone rings and the caller ID shows it's a broker calling about an industrial account—like, any industrial account, even a totally standard manufacturing facility or a distribution center that has nothing to do with the one I visited—I just let it go straight to voicemail. I can't answer it. Um, constantly on the lookout? Like being hyper-aware of things? No, I don't think I'm doing that. I mean, I'm definitely stressed about the work stuff, like I said, with the files and the phone calls and all the industrial accounts just completely freaking me out. And when I'm out walking Barnaby—well, I don't walk Barnaby, he's a cat, he stays inside—but when I'm walking myself to get coffee at the little place down the street, I'm just thinking about what kind of pastry I want, or maybe thinking about the book I'm reading, the Tudor one. I'm not looking at the cars driving by and worrying they're going to swerve onto the sidewalk, or looking at the buildings and worrying something's going to fall off them. 

I feel perfectly safe in my neighborhood and in my apartment. It's really just the very specific trigger of those warehouse files and the thought of having to deal with that kind of risk assessment again that gets me going. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm on the lookout for threats in my everyday life. I'm just trying to get through my normal routine without having to think about that specific Tuesday morning. I get into bed, and I am so physically exhausted, but I just lie there staring at the ceiling. I was actually in the rear loading dock area of the building, just, you know, checking the clearance around the electrical panels because that's a huge compliance issue we always have to look for. The part in between is just gone. No matter how hard I try to piece it together, it's just a total void. And it's so incredibly frustrating because I am a very detail-oriented person, you know? My entire career is based on documenting details, so not knowing how I escaped or what I saw or what I did during those minutes... it just really, really bothers me. I mean, did I run? Did someone grab my arm and pull me out? Did I fall down? I just don't know, and I keep racking my brain trying to find even just a tiny flash of a memory from getting out of that building, but I can't.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding anything related to the warehouse inspection or your work since the incident?\n\nPatient: Well, um... yes, actually. I mean, my boss has been pretty understanding, giving me a few days to get myself together, but the truth is, I haven't even been able to look at the file for that warehouse. It's sitting right there on my home office desk—well, not right on the desk, I actually moved it to the bottom drawer because just seeing the manila folder sitting there was making me feel sick. And it's not just that specific file, either. It's like... anything even remotely connected to it. \n\nI've been letting all my calls about any of our industrial accounts just go straight to voicemail. Which is terrible, I know, because I pride myself on being incredibly responsive to my clients. Usually I return a call within the hour, always. But right now, even just seeing an email pop up on my phone with a subject line about a commercial site visit or a factory policy... my stomach just completely drops. It's this awful, heavy feeling, like I'm on a roller coaster right before it goes over the edge. I just can't bring myself to click on them. I've been marking them as unread and telling myself I'll deal with them later, but then later comes and I just... I can't do it. I just can't open them. It's like my brain is just screaming at me to stay away from anything that reminds me of that place or that kind of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're experiencing any moments where you feel detached from yourself or your surroundings, like you're observing things from outside your body?\n\nPatient: Um, detached? Like an out-of-body thing? I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I've heard people talk about that, you know, in movies or whatever, where they float above themselves or feel like they're in a dream, but I haven't really had anything like that. I'm definitely very stuck in my own body right now, unfortunately. I mean, I feel every little thing. Every time my heart races or my stomach drops or my palms get sweaty, I am acutely aware of it. It's almost the opposite of being detached, really. I feel like I'm hyper-attached to every single uncomfortable physical sensation I'm having. \n\nLike yesterday, for example, I was just sitting in my kitchen, trying to drink some chamomile tea because my mom always said chamomile is good for your nerves, right? And I was just holding the mug, trying to focus on the warmth of it, but instead, I just felt this intense tightness in my chest, and my breathing felt so shallow, and I was just so hyper-focused on how awful I felt in my own skin. So no, I wouldn't say I feel outside of myself at all. I just feel... trapped inside a version of myself that is constantly on edge and exhausted. I just wish I could detach, honestly, just for a little while, just to get a break from feeling so keyed up all the time. But no, I haven't experienced anything like what you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I... no, I wouldn't say guilty. I mean, I've definitely thought about everything leading up to it, playing it over and over in my head. Like, I keep wondering if I had parked in the front lot instead of pulling around to the side, or if I had spent ten more minutes talking to the facility manager about their sprinkler maintenance logs before heading back to the loading dock... would things have been different? Would I have not been right there when it happened? But I don't feel like it was my fault, if that's what you mean. \n\nI know I was just doing my job. I was just following my standard inspection checklist, the exact same one I've used for, gosh, probably eighty or ninety site visits over the last three years. It's just a standard form, you know? You check the electrical panels, you check the fire exits, you check the storage of combustible materials. I was doing exactly what I was supposed to be doing, exactly where I was supposed to be doing it. So, logically, I know there's no reason for me to feel guilty. I didn't cause the blast. I was just... there. \n\nIt's more just this overwhelming sense of frustration about the missing time, like I told you. And maybe a little bit of anger, honestly, that this normal, routine Tuesday morning turned into this huge thing that's completely derailed my life right now. But guilt? No. I don't feel like I did anything wrong. I just feel like I was in the wrong place at the absolute wrong time, and now I'm the one having to deal with the fallout of it, sitting here feeling like a nervous wreck every time I even think about my job. But I definitely don't feel like I'm to blame for any of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to concentrate on your daily activities because of worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um, no, actually. I mean, not on my normal, everyday stuff. It's weird, right? You'd think with everything else going on, my brain would just be complete mush, but I've actually been... well, if anything, I've been throwing myself into my daily chores, and my focus there is perfectly fine. \n\nLike yesterday, I decided to deep clean my entire apartment. And I don't just mean a quick vacuum and wiping down the counters. I mean, I pulled the refrigerator out from the wall to sweep behind it, I scrubbed the grout in the bathroom tile with this little old toothbrush I keep just for cleaning, I reorganized my entire pantry... which honestly took like three hours because I decided to sort everything by expiration date and then by food group. I had all these canned goods from last year, like four different types of diced tomatoes, that I needed to sort through and consolidate. \n\nAnd while I was doing all of that, I was completely focused. I was listening to this podcast about historical European architecture—which is completely unrelated to my job, obviously, since I just underwrite modern commercial properties, I don't design them—and I was following along with every single word the host was saying. I didn't lose my train of thought or forget what I was doing or get distracted by worrying about the incident. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I'm having trouble concentrating on my regular daily activities. As long as it has absolutely nothing to do with work or... or that inspection, my focus is actually completely normal. It's almost like my brain is just so grateful to have something mundane to latch onto, you know? Like, \"Oh, thank goodness, we're just organizing spices right now, let's put a hundred percent of our energy into making sure the paprika is next to the cayenne.\" So yeah, the anxiety isn't really getting in the way of my daily routine stuff at home at all. I can read my books, I can cook complicated recipes, I can pay my personal utility bills... I'm totally dialed in on all of that. It's just the work stuff I can't look at.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself becoming excessively anxious when you encounter reminders of the warehouse inspection, such as seeing a similar building or hearing a loud noise?\n\nPatient: Well, reminders... um, it's not even necessarily seeing a similar building or hearing a loud noise. I mean, I've honestly been avoiding industrial parks and loud places anyway, so I haven't really tested that out too much. It's actually the paperwork that does it. \n\nLike, during the day, I'll just be trying to catch up on things, right? I'll be reviewing a standard policy or looking at a routine loss report for a completely different client—you know, just normal commercial property underwriting stuff, maybe a minor water damage claim or a basic liability renewal for a strip mall or something totally mundane like that. I'm just looking at the text on my screen. \n\nBut out of nowhere, I just suddenly get hit with this incredibly sharp image of what happened. It's so vivid, it's almost like a flash but it just takes over my whole brain. I see the worker on the floor, and all that soot... God, the soot hanging in the air, it's like I can almost taste it and smell the smoke all over again. And it feels like my body just reacts before I can even stop it or tell myself that I'm safe in my own apartment. \n\nMy heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and I get that awful sweaty, tight feeling, and I just completely freeze up. It's so incredibly frustrating because I'm just trying to read a completely unrelated document about a building in, like, Ohio or somewhere. It has nothing to do with warehouses! But boom, suddenly I'm right back there seeing the soot and the worker on the ground. I try to push it away, I really do. I'll minimize the window and just stare at my desktop wallpaper—it's a picture of my cat, Barnaby—but it takes me a good fifteen or twenty minutes to get my breathing back to normal and convince my body that we aren't in danger. So yeah, I guess those work documents are my reminders, and I definitely get... well, excessively anxious is probably an understatement when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the warehouse inspection incident as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Reliving it? Like, like I'm actually back there? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I told you about the images, right? When I'm looking at the paperwork and I get that really sharp flash of the soot and the worker on the floor. That's definitely distressing, and it makes my heart race and my palms sweat, but I know I'm sitting at my desk in my apartment. I know I'm looking at my computer monitor. \n\nIt's not like... it's not like one of those movies where the character suddenly thinks they're back in a war zone and they dive under the table to take cover. I'm not hallucinating or losing touch with reality. I know I'm safe at home with Barnaby. It's just a really, really vivid memory that forces its way in. It's like my brain pulls up a high-definition photograph of that exact moment and shoves it right in front of my face, and my body reacts to the stress of that memory. But I don't feel like I've actually time-traveled back to that Tuesday morning. \n\nI'm very aware that it's just a memory, even if it's an incredibly intrusive and upsetting one. So, no, I don't feel like it's happening all over again in real time. It's just... it's just a very persistent, very detailed recollection that I can't seem to control when I'm trying to review a simple liability policy or a loss report. But I'm definitely still in the present moment when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific people, places, or activities that you're avoiding because they remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Well, avoiding... yeah, absolutely. I mean, I mentioned earlier that I haven't been going near industrial parks, but it's really the work itself that I'm completely dodging. It's terrible, I know it's terrible for my job, but I just can't help it. \n\nLike, the specific warehouse file from the inspection? I haven't opened it once since that day. It's just sitting there on my desktop, in this little folder I made called \"Pending Underwriting,\" and every time my mouse even hovers near it, I feel sick. And it's not just that one file, either. It's spilled over into everything related to it. Any time my phone rings and the caller ID shows it's a broker calling about an industrial account—like, any industrial account, even a totally standard manufacturing facility or a distribution center that has nothing to do with the one I visited—I just let it go straight to voicemail. I can't answer it. I'll sit there and watch the little red light blink on my desk phone, and I'll tell myself, \"Just pick it up, it's just a conversation about sprinkler systems and square footage,\" but my hand just won't move. \n\nAnd the emails... oh my gosh, the emails are almost worse because they just pile up in my inbox, right? I'll see a subject line that says something like \"Warehouse Liability Renewal\" or \"Industrial Property Quote,\" and my stomach just completely drops. Like, physically drops, like I'm on a roller coaster that just went over the edge. I get this awful, heavy feeling right in the pit of my stomach, and I just quickly click on a different email, maybe something about a small retail shop or a suburban office building, just to get it off my screen. \n\nI know I'm going to have to deal with it eventually, because my manager is going to start asking why these accounts are stalling, and I don't know what I'm going to tell her. \"Sorry, Susan, I can't underwrite warehouses anymore because they make my stomach hurt\"? She's not going to accept that. But right now, I'm just doing everything in my power to avoid anything that even tangentially reminds me of that industrial space. It's exhausting, honestly, trying to navigate my own inbox like it's a minefield. So yes, specific activities—definitely opening those files and taking those calls. I'm avoiding them like the plague.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um, lost interest? No, not really. I mean, I still like doing the things I usually do. Like, I still read a lot. I'm actually in the middle of this really long historical fiction novel right now, set in the Tudor period, and I'm still really into it. I'll sit down on the couch with Barnaby—my cat, I mentioned him earlier—and I'll read for a couple of hours in the evening. And I still enjoy cooking, too. I made this really complicated mushroom risotto from scratch on Saturday, stirring it constantly for like forty-five minutes just to get the arborio rice perfectly creamy, and I really enjoyed the process and eating it afterward. \n\nAnd I still like watching my shows. I have this reality TV competition I watch every Wednesday night with a glass of wine, and I'm still totally invested in who gets eliminated. So, no, I wouldn't say I've lost interest in my hobbies or anything. I still look forward to those things. It's just the work stuff, really, that's completely overwhelming me right now. But my personal life, the things I do for fun... those are still pretty much the same. I still get enjoyment out of them, thank goodness, because I don't know what I'd do if I couldn't even enjoy a good book or a bowl of risotto right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not in a situation that would normally be considered dangerous?\n\nPatient: Um, constantly on the lookout? Like being hyper-aware of things? No, I don't think I'm doing that. I mean, I'm definitely stressed about the work stuff, like I said, with the files and the phone calls and all the industrial accounts just completely freaking me out. But when I'm just going about my normal day, like walking to the grocery store or sitting in my apartment, I'm not, like, scanning the room for danger or anything like that. \n\nI'm not jumping at shadows or checking the locks on my door five times before I go to bed. I just lock it once like I always do. And when I'm out walking Barnaby—well, I don't walk Barnaby, he's a cat, he stays inside—but when I'm walking myself to get coffee at the little place down the street, I'm just thinking about what kind of pastry I want, or maybe thinking about the book I'm reading, the Tudor one. I'm not looking at the cars driving by and worrying they're going to swerve onto the sidewalk, or looking at the buildings and worrying something's going to fall off them. \n\nI feel perfectly safe in my neighborhood and in my apartment. It's really just the very specific trigger of those warehouse files and the thought of having to deal with that kind of risk assessment again that gets me going. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm on the lookout for threats in my everyday life. I'm just trying to get through my normal routine without having to think about that specific Tuesday morning. But otherwise, I feel pretty secure in my surroundings. I'm not on edge about general danger, just... just the specific work things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, startled... yes. Yes, definitely. It's actually gotten really embarrassing at the office. I mean, I know I just said I'm not scanning for danger when I'm walking down the street, and that's true, but when something sudden actually happens, my reaction is just completely out of proportion. 

Like, the other day, I was sitting at my desk—just trying to avoid my inbox, like I told you—and the big communal printer right outside my cubicle jammed. It made this loud, grinding *clack* noise, which it does all the time, honestly, it's a terrible machine. But this time, when it happened, I practically leaped out of my chair. I gasped so loud that my coworker, David, popped his head over the partition to ask if I was okay. And my heart was just hammering in my chest, like, beating so fast I could feel it in my throat, and I felt this rush of cold sweat. It took me... I don't know, maybe ten or fifteen minutes just to get my breathing back to normal and stop my hands from shaking. Over a printer jam! 

And it's not just loud noises, either. If someone just walks up behind my desk while I'm focused on my screen... like, Susan, my manager, she has this habit of just appearing behind you and saying, "Hey, got a second?" And usually, I'd just turn around and say sure. But yesterday she did it, and I jumped so hard I knocked my water bottle over. It spilled all over my desk pad, and I was just sitting there, completely panicked, feeling like something awful was right behind me, even though it was just Susan holding a file folder. I had to excuse myself to the restroom to just sit in a stall and take deep breaths until the adrenaline wore off. 

So, yeah, any sudden noise—a door slamming down the hall, a heavy binder getting dropped on a desk, someone clearing their throat too loudly when I'm not expecting it—I just jump out of my skin. It's like my body is just primed to react to any sudden change in the environment, even if my brain knows I'm perfectly safe in a carpeted office building. It's really exhausting, having to calm myself down from these massive adrenaline spikes multiple times a day over completely normal office sounds.

I was actually in the rear loading dock area of the building, just, you know, checking the clearance around the electrical panels because that's a huge compliance issue we always have to look for. The part in between is just gone. No matter how hard I try to piece it together, it's just a total void. And it's so incredibly frustrating because I am a very detail-oriented person, you know? My entire career is based on documenting details, so not knowing how I escaped or what I saw or what I did during those minutes... it just really, really bothers me. I mean, did I run? Did someone grab my arm and pull me out? Did I fall down? I just don't know, and I keep racking my brain trying to find even just a tiny flash of a memory from getting out of that building, but I can't.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding anything related to the warehouse inspection or your work since the incident?\n\nPatient: Well, um... yes, actually. I mean, my boss has been pretty understanding, giving me a few days to get myself together, but the truth is, I haven't even been able to look at the file for that warehouse. It's sitting right there on my home office desk—well, not right on the desk, I actually moved it to the bottom drawer because just seeing the manila folder sitting there was making me feel sick. And it's not just that specific file, either. It's like... anything even remotely connected to it. \n\nI've been letting all my calls about any of our industrial accounts just go straight to voicemail. Which is terrible, I know, because I pride myself on being incredibly responsive to my clients. Usually I return a call within the hour, always. But right now, even just seeing an email pop up on my phone with a subject line about a commercial site visit or a factory policy... my stomach just completely drops. It's this awful, heavy feeling, like I'm on a roller coaster right before it goes over the edge. I just can't bring myself to click on them. I've been marking them as unread and telling myself I'll deal with them later, but then later comes and I just... I can't do it. I just can't open them. It's like my brain is just screaming at me to stay away from anything that reminds me of that place or that kind of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're experiencing any moments where you feel detached from yourself or your surroundings, like you're observing things from outside your body?\n\nPatient: Um, detached? Like an out-of-body thing? I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I've heard people talk about that, you know, in movies or whatever, where they float above themselves or feel like they're in a dream, but I haven't really had anything like that. I'm definitely very stuck in my own body right now, unfortunately. I mean, I feel every little thing. Every time my heart races or my stomach drops or my palms get sweaty, I am acutely aware of it. It's almost the opposite of being detached, really. I feel like I'm hyper-attached to every single uncomfortable physical sensation I'm having. \n\nLike yesterday, for example, I was just sitting in my kitchen, trying to drink some chamomile tea because my mom always said chamomile is good for your nerves, right? And I was just holding the mug, trying to focus on the warmth of it, but instead, I just felt this intense tightness in my chest, and my breathing felt so shallow, and I was just so hyper-focused on how awful I felt in my own skin. So no, I wouldn't say I feel outside of myself at all. I just feel... trapped inside a version of myself that is constantly on edge and exhausted. I just wish I could detach, honestly, just for a little while, just to get a break from feeling so keyed up all the time. But no, I haven't experienced anything like what you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I... no, I wouldn't say guilty. I mean, I've definitely thought about everything leading up to it, playing it over and over in my head. Like, I keep wondering if I had parked in the front lot instead of pulling around to the side, or if I had spent ten more minutes talking to the facility manager about their sprinkler maintenance logs before heading back to the loading dock... would things have been different? Would I have not been right there when it happened? But I don't feel like it was my fault, if that's what you mean. \n\nI know I was just doing my job. I was just following my standard inspection checklist, the exact same one I've used for, gosh, probably eighty or ninety site visits over the last three years. It's just a standard form, you know? You check the electrical panels, you check the fire exits, you check the storage of combustible materials. I was doing exactly what I was supposed to be doing, exactly where I was supposed to be doing it. So, logically, I know there's no reason for me to feel guilty. I didn't cause the blast. I was just... there. \n\nIt's more just this overwhelming sense of frustration about the missing time, like I told you. And maybe a little bit of anger, honestly, that this normal, routine Tuesday morning turned into this huge thing that's completely derailed my life right now. But guilt? No. I don't feel like I did anything wrong. I just feel like I was in the wrong place at the absolute wrong time, and now I'm the one having to deal with the fallout of it, sitting here feeling like a nervous wreck every time I even think about my job. But I definitely don't feel like I'm to blame for any of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to concentrate on your daily activities because of worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um, no, actually. I mean, not on my normal, everyday stuff. It's weird, right? You'd think with everything else going on, my brain would just be complete mush, but I've actually been... well, if anything, I've been throwing myself into my daily chores, and my focus there is perfectly fine. \n\nLike yesterday, I decided to deep clean my entire apartment. And I don't just mean a quick vacuum and wiping down the counters. I mean, I pulled the refrigerator out from the wall to sweep behind it, I scrubbed the grout in the bathroom tile with this little old toothbrush I keep just for cleaning, I reorganized my entire pantry... which honestly took like three hours because I decided to sort everything by expiration date and then by food group. I had all these canned goods from last year, like four different types of diced tomatoes, that I needed to sort through and consolidate. \n\nAnd while I was doing all of that, I was completely focused. I was listening to this podcast about historical European architecture—which is completely unrelated to my job, obviously, since I just underwrite modern commercial properties, I don't design them—and I was following along with every single word the host was saying. I didn't lose my train of thought or forget what I was doing or get distracted by worrying about the incident. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I'm having trouble concentrating on my regular daily activities. As long as it has absolutely nothing to do with work or... or that inspection, my focus is actually completely normal. It's almost like my brain is just so grateful to have something mundane to latch onto, you know? Like, \"Oh, thank goodness, we're just organizing spices right now, let's put a hundred percent of our energy into making sure the paprika is next to the cayenne.\" So yeah, the anxiety isn't really getting in the way of my daily routine stuff at home at all. I can read my books, I can cook complicated recipes, I can pay my personal utility bills... I'm totally dialed in on all of that. It's just the work stuff I can't look at.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself becoming excessively anxious when you encounter reminders of the warehouse inspection, such as seeing a similar building or hearing a loud noise?\n\nPatient: Well, reminders... um, it's not even necessarily seeing a similar building or hearing a loud noise. I mean, I've honestly been avoiding industrial parks and loud places anyway, so I haven't really tested that out too much. It's actually the paperwork that does it. \n\nLike, during the day, I'll just be trying to catch up on things, right? I'll be reviewing a standard policy or looking at a routine loss report for a completely different client—you know, just normal commercial property underwriting stuff, maybe a minor water damage claim or a basic liability renewal for a strip mall or something totally mundane like that. I'm just looking at the text on my screen. \n\nBut out of nowhere, I just suddenly get hit with this incredibly sharp image of what happened. It's so vivid, it's almost like a flash but it just takes over my whole brain. I see the worker on the floor, and all that soot... God, the soot hanging in the air, it's like I can almost taste it and smell the smoke all over again. And it feels like my body just reacts before I can even stop it or tell myself that I'm safe in my own apartment. \n\nMy heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and I get that awful sweaty, tight feeling, and I just completely freeze up. It's so incredibly frustrating because I'm just trying to read a completely unrelated document about a building in, like, Ohio or somewhere. It has nothing to do with warehouses! But boom, suddenly I'm right back there seeing the soot and the worker on the ground. I try to push it away, I really do. I'll minimize the window and just stare at my desktop wallpaper—it's a picture of my cat, Barnaby—but it takes me a good fifteen or twenty minutes to get my breathing back to normal and convince my body that we aren't in danger. So yeah, I guess those work documents are my reminders, and I definitely get... well, excessively anxious is probably an understatement when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the warehouse inspection incident as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Reliving it? Like, like I'm actually back there? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I told you about the images, right? When I'm looking at the paperwork and I get that really sharp flash of the soot and the worker on the floor. That's definitely distressing, and it makes my heart race and my palms sweat, but I know I'm sitting at my desk in my apartment. I know I'm looking at my computer monitor. \n\nIt's not like... it's not like one of those movies where the character suddenly thinks they're back in a war zone and they dive under the table to take cover. I'm not hallucinating or losing touch with reality. I know I'm safe at home with Barnaby. It's just a really, really vivid memory that forces its way in. It's like my brain pulls up a high-definition photograph of that exact moment and shoves it right in front of my face, and my body reacts to the stress of that memory. But I don't feel like I've actually time-traveled back to that Tuesday morning. \n\nI'm very aware that it's just a memory, even if it's an incredibly intrusive and upsetting one. So, no, I don't feel like it's happening all over again in real time. It's just... it's just a very persistent, very detailed recollection that I can't seem to control when I'm trying to review a simple liability policy or a loss report. But I'm definitely still in the present moment when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific people, places, or activities that you're avoiding because they remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Well, avoiding... yeah, absolutely. I mean, I mentioned earlier that I haven't been going near industrial parks, but it's really the work itself that I'm completely dodging. It's terrible, I know it's terrible for my job, but I just can't help it. \n\nLike, the specific warehouse file from the inspection? I haven't opened it once since that day. It's just sitting there on my desktop, in this little folder I made called \"Pending Underwriting,\" and every time my mouse even hovers near it, I feel sick. And it's not just that one file, either. It's spilled over into everything related to it. Any time my phone rings and the caller ID shows it's a broker calling about an industrial account—like, any industrial account, even a totally standard manufacturing facility or a distribution center that has nothing to do with the one I visited—I just let it go straight to voicemail. I can't answer it. I'll sit there and watch the little red light blink on my desk phone, and I'll tell myself, \"Just pick it up, it's just a conversation about sprinkler systems and square footage,\" but my hand just won't move. \n\nAnd the emails... oh my gosh, the emails are almost worse because they just pile up in my inbox, right? I'll see a subject line that says something like \"Warehouse Liability Renewal\" or \"Industrial Property Quote,\" and my stomach just completely drops. Like, physically drops, like I'm on a roller coaster that just went over the edge. I get this awful, heavy feeling right in the pit of my stomach, and I just quickly click on a different email, maybe something about a small retail shop or a suburban office building, just to get it off my screen. \n\nI know I'm going to have to deal with it eventually, because my manager is going to start asking why these accounts are stalling, and I don't know what I'm going to tell her. \"Sorry, Susan, I can't underwrite warehouses anymore because they make my stomach hurt\"? She's not going to accept that. But right now, I'm just doing everything in my power to avoid anything that even tangentially reminds me of that industrial space. It's exhausting, honestly, trying to navigate my own inbox like it's a minefield. So yes, specific activities—definitely opening those files and taking those calls. I'm avoiding them like the plague.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um, lost interest? No, not really. I mean, I still like doing the things I usually do. Like, I still read a lot. I'm actually in the middle of this really long historical fiction novel right now, set in the Tudor period, and I'm still really into it. I'll sit down on the couch with Barnaby—my cat, I mentioned him earlier—and I'll read for a couple of hours in the evening. And I still enjoy cooking, too. I made this really complicated mushroom risotto from scratch on Saturday, stirring it constantly for like forty-five minutes just to get the arborio rice perfectly creamy, and I really enjoyed the process and eating it afterward. \n\nAnd I still like watching my shows. I have this reality TV competition I watch every Wednesday night with a glass of wine, and I'm still totally invested in who gets eliminated. So, no, I wouldn't say I've lost interest in my hobbies or anything. I still look forward to those things. It's just the work stuff, really, that's completely overwhelming me right now. But my personal life, the things I do for fun... those are still pretty much the same. I still get enjoyment out of them, thank goodness, because I don't know what I'd do if I couldn't even enjoy a good book or a bowl of risotto right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not in a situation that would normally be considered dangerous?\n\nPatient: Um, constantly on the lookout? Like being hyper-aware of things? No, I don't think I'm doing that. I mean, I'm definitely stressed about the work stuff, like I said, with the files and the phone calls and all the industrial accounts just completely freaking me out. But when I'm just going about my normal day, like walking to the grocery store or sitting in my apartment, I'm not, like, scanning the room for danger or anything like that. \n\nI'm not jumping at shadows or checking the locks on my door five times before I go to bed. I just lock it once like I always do. And when I'm out walking Barnaby—well, I don't walk Barnaby, he's a cat, he stays inside—but when I'm walking myself to get coffee at the little place down the street, I'm just thinking about what kind of pastry I want, or maybe thinking about the book I'm reading, the Tudor one. I'm not looking at the cars driving by and worrying they're going to swerve onto the sidewalk, or looking at the buildings and worrying something's going to fall off them. \n\nI feel perfectly safe in my neighborhood and in my apartment. It's really just the very specific trigger of those warehouse files and the thought of having to deal with that kind of risk assessment again that gets me going. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm on the lookout for threats in my everyday life. I'm just trying to get through my normal routine without having to think about that specific Tuesday morning. But otherwise, I feel pretty secure in my surroundings. I'm not on edge about general danger, just... just the specific work things.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself startled more easily than you used to, such as being frightened by a sudden noise?\n\nPatient: Oh, startled... yes. Yes, definitely. It's actually gotten really embarrassing at the office. I mean, I know I just said I'm not scanning for danger when I'm walking down the street, and that's true, but when something sudden actually happens, my reaction is just completely out of proportion. \n\nLike, the other day, I was sitting at my desk—just trying to avoid my inbox, like I told you—and the big communal printer right outside my cubicle jammed. It made this loud, grinding *clack* noise, which it does all the time, honestly, it's a terrible machine. But this time, when it happened, I practically leaped out of my chair. I gasped so loud that my coworker, David, popped his head over the partition to ask if I was okay. And my heart was just hammering in my chest, like, beating so fast I could feel it in my throat, and I felt this rush of cold sweat. It took me... I don't know, maybe ten or fifteen minutes just to get my breathing back to normal and stop my hands from shaking. Over a printer jam! \n\nAnd it's not just loud noises, either. If someone just walks up behind my desk while I'm focused on my screen... like, Susan, my manager, she has this habit of just appearing behind you and saying, \"Hey, got a second?\" And usually, I'd just turn around and say sure. But yesterday she did it, and I jumped so hard I knocked my water bottle over. It spilled all over my desk pad, and I was just sitting there, completely panicked, feeling like something awful was right behind me, even though it was just Susan holding a file folder. I had to excuse myself to the restroom to just sit in a stall and take deep breaths until the adrenaline wore off. \n\nSo, yeah, any sudden noise—a door slamming down the hall, a heavy binder getting dropped on a desk, someone clearing their throat too loudly when I'm not expecting it—I just jump out of my skin. It's like my body is just primed to react to any sudden change in the environment, even if my brain knows I'm perfectly safe in a carpeted office building. It's really exhausting, having to calm myself down from these massive adrenaline spikes multiple times a day over completely normal office sounds.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Reliving it... yeah. Yes, I do. It's... it's really disturbing, honestly. I don't know if I'm, like, hallucinating exactly, not like I think I'm actually standing in a different room, but it absolutely feels like I'm pulled right back into that exact moment. 

Like, during the day, I’ll just be sitting at my desk—doing exactly what I'm supposed to be doing, just trying to get through my queue. I might be reviewing a standard commercial property policy, or maybe I'm looking at a routine loss report for a totally different client, something completely unrelated to that specific industrial account. Just normal underwriter stuff. And then, completely out of nowhere, I'll suddenly get hit with this incredibly sharp, vivid image of the warehouse. 

I see the worker, the one who got hurt, just lying there on the concrete floor, exactly how it looked, and I can actually see the soot hanging in the air. It's so clear, like a movie playing right over my actual vision. And the worst part is that it feels like my body reacts before I can even stop it or tell myself it's not real. My stomach drops, my chest seizes up, and I get that exact same rush of pure panic I felt that day. It's like my physical body is transported right back into that awful, ruined building, smelling the smoke and the chemicals, even though I'm just staring at a PDF on my monitor in a perfectly safe, climate-controlled office. 

I have to literally grip the edges of my desk and close my eyes and force myself to realize where I am, because for that split second, the image is just so overpowering. It just hijacks my brain completely. I can't predict when it's going to happen, either. It could be triggered by a specific word in a report, or maybe nothing at all, just... bam, there it is again, the soot, the floor, the whole awful thing, and I'm right back in the middle of it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Um, well, I came in today because... honestly, I just haven’t been sleeping. Barely at all, really. It’s been like this ever since I had to do this warehouse inspection for work. I’m an insurance underwriter, so going out to commercial sites is just a normal part of my job, you know? Usually it's completely routine, just checking off liability boxes and taking photos. But ever since that specific inspection, I just... I get into bed, and I am so physically exhausted, but I just lie there staring at the ceiling. My mind just keeps replaying these little bits and pieces of what happened, over and over, like a terrible video loop I can't pause or turn off. \n\nAnd then, on the rare chance that I actually do manage to drift off for a few minutes, it doesn’t even last. I wake up completely drenched in sweat, just wide awake and gasping. It’s this horrible, visceral feeling, exactly like I’m right back there near that blast again. And once that happens, the whole night is just ruined because I'm too wired to even try closing my eyes again, so I just sit there in the dark thinking about the warehouse and how I felt, and I just... I really need to get some sleep.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the blast you mentioned?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um... so, like I said, it was just a normal commercial site visit. I was actually in the rear loading dock area of the building, just, you know, checking the clearance around the electrical panels because that's a huge compliance issue we always have to look for. I had my clipboard, and I was just writing down some notes about the fire doors, completely focused on my paperwork. And then... I just remember hearing it. It wasn't even just a sound, it was like this massive, deafening roar that just rattled my teeth and vibrated right through my chest. \n\nBut the thing that is driving me absolutely crazy—and I mean, I have sat up for hours just trying to force myself to figure this out—is that right after that sound, there is just... nothing. It's completely blank. Like someone just cut the film. The very next thing I know, I am standing all the way out in the gravel parking lot, way past the front chain-link fence. And I'm looking down, and there is this thick, dark soot just caked all over the sleeves and the front of my jacket. It was this beige blazer I wear to all my client meetings, completely ruined. \n\nAnd I'm standing out there, and people are yelling, but I have absolutely no idea how I got from the loading dock to the parking lot. The part in between is just gone. No matter how hard I try to piece it together, it's just a total void. And it's so incredibly frustrating because I am a very detail-oriented person, you know? My entire career is based on documenting details, so not knowing how I escaped or what I saw or what I did during those minutes... it just really, really bothers me. I mean, did I run? Did someone grab my arm and pull me out? Did I fall down? I just don't know, and I keep racking my brain trying to find even just a tiny flash of a memory from getting out of that building, but I can't.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding anything related to the warehouse inspection or your work since the incident?\n\nPatient: Well, um... yes, actually. I mean, my boss has been pretty understanding, giving me a few days to get myself together, but the truth is, I haven't even been able to look at the file for that warehouse. It's sitting right there on my home office desk—well, not right on the desk, I actually moved it to the bottom drawer because just seeing the manila folder sitting there was making me feel sick. And it's not just that specific file, either. It's like... anything even remotely connected to it. \n\nI've been letting all my calls about any of our industrial accounts just go straight to voicemail. Which is terrible, I know, because I pride myself on being incredibly responsive to my clients. Usually I return a call within the hour, always. But right now, even just seeing an email pop up on my phone with a subject line about a commercial site visit or a factory policy... my stomach just completely drops. It's this awful, heavy feeling, like I'm on a roller coaster right before it goes over the edge. I just can't bring myself to click on them. I've been marking them as unread and telling myself I'll deal with them later, but then later comes and I just... I can't do it. I just can't open them. It's like my brain is just screaming at me to stay away from anything that reminds me of that place or that kind of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're experiencing any moments where you feel detached from yourself or your surroundings, like you're observing things from outside your body?\n\nPatient: Um, detached? Like an out-of-body thing? I don't... I don't think so. I mean, I've heard people talk about that, you know, in movies or whatever, where they float above themselves or feel like they're in a dream, but I haven't really had anything like that. I'm definitely very stuck in my own body right now, unfortunately. I mean, I feel every little thing. Every time my heart races or my stomach drops or my palms get sweaty, I am acutely aware of it. It's almost the opposite of being detached, really. I feel like I'm hyper-attached to every single uncomfortable physical sensation I'm having. \n\nLike yesterday, for example, I was just sitting in my kitchen, trying to drink some chamomile tea because my mom always said chamomile is good for your nerves, right? And I was just holding the mug, trying to focus on the warmth of it, but instead, I just felt this intense tightness in my chest, and my breathing felt so shallow, and I was just so hyper-focused on how awful I felt in my own skin. So no, I wouldn't say I feel outside of myself at all. I just feel... trapped inside a version of myself that is constantly on edge and exhausted. I just wish I could detach, honestly, just for a little while, just to get a break from feeling so keyed up all the time. But no, I haven't experienced anything like what you're describing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened during the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I... no, I wouldn't say guilty. I mean, I've definitely thought about everything leading up to it, playing it over and over in my head. Like, I keep wondering if I had parked in the front lot instead of pulling around to the side, or if I had spent ten more minutes talking to the facility manager about their sprinkler maintenance logs before heading back to the loading dock... would things have been different? Would I have not been right there when it happened? But I don't feel like it was my fault, if that's what you mean. \n\nI know I was just doing my job. I was just following my standard inspection checklist, the exact same one I've used for, gosh, probably eighty or ninety site visits over the last three years. It's just a standard form, you know? You check the electrical panels, you check the fire exits, you check the storage of combustible materials. I was doing exactly what I was supposed to be doing, exactly where I was supposed to be doing it. So, logically, I know there's no reason for me to feel guilty. I didn't cause the blast. I was just... there. \n\nIt's more just this overwhelming sense of frustration about the missing time, like I told you. And maybe a little bit of anger, honestly, that this normal, routine Tuesday morning turned into this huge thing that's completely derailed my life right now. But guilt? No. I don't feel like I did anything wrong. I just feel like I was in the wrong place at the absolute wrong time, and now I'm the one having to deal with the fallout of it, sitting here feeling like a nervous wreck every time I even think about my job. But I definitely don't feel like I'm to blame for any of it.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to concentrate on your daily activities because of worry or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um, no, actually. I mean, not on my normal, everyday stuff. It's weird, right? You'd think with everything else going on, my brain would just be complete mush, but I've actually been... well, if anything, I've been throwing myself into my daily chores, and my focus there is perfectly fine. \n\nLike yesterday, I decided to deep clean my entire apartment. And I don't just mean a quick vacuum and wiping down the counters. I mean, I pulled the refrigerator out from the wall to sweep behind it, I scrubbed the grout in the bathroom tile with this little old toothbrush I keep just for cleaning, I reorganized my entire pantry... which honestly took like three hours because I decided to sort everything by expiration date and then by food group. I had all these canned goods from last year, like four different types of diced tomatoes, that I needed to sort through and consolidate. \n\nAnd while I was doing all of that, I was completely focused. I was listening to this podcast about historical European architecture—which is completely unrelated to my job, obviously, since I just underwrite modern commercial properties, I don't design them—and I was following along with every single word the host was saying. I didn't lose my train of thought or forget what I was doing or get distracted by worrying about the incident. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I'm having trouble concentrating on my regular daily activities. As long as it has absolutely nothing to do with work or... or that inspection, my focus is actually completely normal. It's almost like my brain is just so grateful to have something mundane to latch onto, you know? Like, \"Oh, thank goodness, we're just organizing spices right now, let's put a hundred percent of our energy into making sure the paprika is next to the cayenne.\" So yeah, the anxiety isn't really getting in the way of my daily routine stuff at home at all. I can read my books, I can cook complicated recipes, I can pay my personal utility bills... I'm totally dialed in on all of that. It's just the work stuff I can't look at.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself becoming excessively anxious when you encounter reminders of the warehouse inspection, such as seeing a similar building or hearing a loud noise?\n\nPatient: Well, reminders... um, it's not even necessarily seeing a similar building or hearing a loud noise. I mean, I've honestly been avoiding industrial parks and loud places anyway, so I haven't really tested that out too much. It's actually the paperwork that does it. \n\nLike, during the day, I'll just be trying to catch up on things, right? I'll be reviewing a standard policy or looking at a routine loss report for a completely different client—you know, just normal commercial property underwriting stuff, maybe a minor water damage claim or a basic liability renewal for a strip mall or something totally mundane like that. I'm just looking at the text on my screen. \n\nBut out of nowhere, I just suddenly get hit with this incredibly sharp image of what happened. It's so vivid, it's almost like a flash but it just takes over my whole brain. I see the worker on the floor, and all that soot... God, the soot hanging in the air, it's like I can almost taste it and smell the smoke all over again. And it feels like my body just reacts before I can even stop it or tell myself that I'm safe in my own apartment. \n\nMy heart just starts pounding out of my chest, and I get that awful sweaty, tight feeling, and I just completely freeze up. It's so incredibly frustrating because I'm just trying to read a completely unrelated document about a building in, like, Ohio or somewhere. It has nothing to do with warehouses! But boom, suddenly I'm right back there seeing the soot and the worker on the ground. I try to push it away, I really do. I'll minimize the window and just stare at my desktop wallpaper—it's a picture of my cat, Barnaby—but it takes me a good fifteen or twenty minutes to get my breathing back to normal and convince my body that we aren't in danger. So yeah, I guess those work documents are my reminders, and I definitely get... well, excessively anxious is probably an understatement when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the warehouse inspection incident as if it were actually happening again?\n\nPatient: Reliving it? Like, like I'm actually back there? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I told you about the images, right? When I'm looking at the paperwork and I get that really sharp flash of the soot and the worker on the floor. That's definitely distressing, and it makes my heart race and my palms sweat, but I know I'm sitting at my desk in my apartment. I know I'm looking at my computer monitor. \n\nIt's not like... it's not like one of those movies where the character suddenly thinks they're back in a war zone and they dive under the table to take cover. I'm not hallucinating or losing touch with reality. I know I'm safe at home with Barnaby. It's just a really, really vivid memory that forces its way in. It's like my brain pulls up a high-definition photograph of that exact moment and shoves it right in front of my face, and my body reacts to the stress of that memory. But I don't feel like I've actually time-traveled back to that Tuesday morning. \n\nI'm very aware that it's just a memory, even if it's an incredibly intrusive and upsetting one. So, no, I don't feel like it's happening all over again in real time. It's just... it's just a very persistent, very detailed recollection that I can't seem to control when I'm trying to review a simple liability policy or a loss report. But I'm definitely still in the present moment when it happens.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific people, places, or activities that you're avoiding because they remind you of the warehouse inspection?\n\nPatient: Well, avoiding... yeah, absolutely. I mean, I mentioned earlier that I haven't been going near industrial parks, but it's really the work itself that I'm completely dodging. It's terrible, I know it's terrible for my job, but I just can't help it. \n\nLike, the specific warehouse file from the inspection? I haven't opened it once since that day. It's just sitting there on my desktop, in this little folder I made called \"Pending Underwriting,\" and every time my mouse even hovers near it, I feel sick. And it's not just that one file, either. It's spilled over into everything related to it. Any time my phone rings and the caller ID shows it's a broker calling about an industrial account—like, any industrial account, even a totally standard manufacturing facility or a distribution center that has nothing to do with the one I visited—I just let it go straight to voicemail. I can't answer it. I'll sit there and watch the little red light blink on my desk phone, and I'll tell myself, \"Just pick it up, it's just a conversation about sprinkler systems and square footage,\" but my hand just won't move. \n\nAnd the emails... oh my gosh, the emails are almost worse because they just pile up in my inbox, right? I'll see a subject line that says something like \"Warehouse Liability Renewal\" or \"Industrial Property Quote,\" and my stomach just completely drops. Like, physically drops, like I'm on a roller coaster that just went over the edge. I get this awful, heavy feeling right in the pit of my stomach, and I just quickly click on a different email, maybe something about a small retail shop or a suburban office building, just to get it off my screen. \n\nI know I'm going to have to deal with it eventually, because my manager is going to start asking why these accounts are stalling, and I don't know what I'm going to tell her. \"Sorry, Susan, I can't underwrite warehouses anymore because they make my stomach hurt\"? She's not going to accept that. But right now, I'm just doing everything in my power to avoid anything that even tangentially reminds me of that industrial space. It's exhausting, honestly, trying to navigate my own inbox like it's a minefield. So yes, specific activities—definitely opening those files and taking those calls. I'm avoiding them like the plague.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Um, lost interest? No, not really. I mean, I still like doing the things I usually do. Like, I still read a lot. I'll sit down on the couch with Barnaby—my cat, I mentioned him earlier—and I'll read for a couple of hours in the evening. And I still enjoy cooking, too. I made this really complicated mushroom risotto from scratch on Saturday, stirring it constantly for like forty-five minutes just to get the arborio rice perfectly creamy, and I really enjoyed the process and eating it afterward. \n\nAnd I still like watching my shows. I have this reality TV competition I watch every Wednesday night with a glass of wine, and I'm still totally invested in who gets eliminated. It's like my body is just primed to react to any sudden change in the environment, even if my brain knows I'm perfectly safe in a carpeted office building. It's really exhausting, having to calm myself down from these massive adrenaline spikes multiple times a day over completely normal office sounds.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the warehouse incident, seeing or hearing things that aren't actually there, as if you're back in that moment?\n\nPatient: Reliving it... yeah.
